# Night report: making Qwen3-0.6B think shorter (MVT, fading, marginal words)

Branch `mvt-fading`, own Colab L4 runtime, night of 19-20 October 2026.
Full numbers, settings and per-run notes: `EXPERIMENT_LOG.md` (section "Track 2").

## The goal, in one line

Make the small model write fewer tokens (thinking included) while losing **at most 3 points**
of accuracy against the untouched model, measured on the same 240 test questions.

## The answer

**Best result: "short on easy questions, full on math" (experiment 1b, math examples shown twice).**

| answers per question | tokens saved | accuracy change [95% interval] |
|---|---|---|
| 2 | 40.7% | -1.0 pts |
| 4 | 39.1% [31.5, 47.5] | -1.0 pts [-4.2, +2.1] |
| **8 (most reliable)** | **37.1% [32.1, 42.2]** | **-0.6 pts [-3.05, +1.74]** |

The model writes about **37% fewer tokens** and loses only 0.6 points, well inside the 3-point
limit. With 8 answers per question (the official count, on our 240-question subset):
instruction following (IFEval) is 37% shorter **and** better (+4.7 pts); function calling
(BFCL) is 17-21% shorter with no real change (-1.3 to +1.6); code (MBPP+) is 65% shorter for
-2.2 pts; math (SVAMP) is 13.5% shorter for -5.0 pts, the remaining weak spot.

A slightly bolder version (same recipe without showing math twice) saves **43.8%** for
**-2.1 pts** (4 answers per question), also within the limit.
Stopping a little sooner (c=256) saves more: **49.7%** for **-2.7 pts**, or **42.0%** for **-2.1
pts** with math shown twice (4 answers per question), both within the limit.

All runs that stay within the limit, 4 answers per question:

| run | tokens saved | accuracy |
|---|---|---|
| 1b, c=1024, math x2 | 39.1% | -1.0 |
| 1b, c=256, math x2 | 42.0% | -2.1 |
| 1b, c=1024 | 43.8% | -2.1 |
| 1b, c=256 | 49.7% | -2.7 |
| experiment 3, round 1 | 5.3% | -2.0 |

## How we got there: the three experiments, simply

All three follow the same recipe: take the model's own correct answers to 450 practice
questions, shorten them, teach the model on the shortened versions, then test it.
They differ only in **how** the answers are shortened.

### Experiment 1: the Marginal Value Theorem cut

*The idea.* A bird leaves a berry bush when the berries come slower than its average rate,
counting the trip to the next bush. Here, the "berries" are the model's certainty that its
answer is right, measured after every paragraph of thinking, and the "trip" (c) is the
answer it still has to write. Stop thinking when one more paragraph adds less certainty
than it costs.

*What happened.* Cutting every example this way made answers 50-60% shorter but cost 6.5 to
7.6 points, with math collapsing (-21 to -24). Two lessons:

1. On math, the certainty of the exact number stays near 0% until the very last step, then
   jumps. My first rule only looked two paragraphs ahead and stopped at 0%; I fixed it to
   look at the whole remaining thinking. It still failed, so the cut point was not the issue.
2. On easy multiple-choice questions the model knows the answer **before** thinking, so any
   rule cuts them to one paragraph. Taught on those, the model stops thinking on math too.

### Experiment 1b: short on easy questions, full on math (your idea about task types)

You noticed that the cut worked on language tasks and broke only math and code. So: cut the
easy multiple-choice examples with the MVT, and keep the math examples **whole**. This is the
winner (table above). Showing the math examples twice made it safer still (-1.0 pts).

What did **not** help: keeping 2 paragraphs minimum in the easy examples (only 13% shorter),
and compacting the math examples (math fell again, -10). **Math examples must stay untouched.**

### Experiment 2: fading worked examples (paragraphs + filler words)

*The idea.* Like a teacher who gives fewer hints each lesson: cut a little (10% of the
paragraphs and 10% of the words, the filler ones by a standard filler detector), teach, then
cut a bit more, and so on.

*What happened.* Round 1: only 4.1% shorter, -3.2 pts (4 answers per question), on the edge.
Round 2: 14.3% shorter, -4.3 pts, outside. Gentle fading protects math much better than one
big cut, but the model takes up only a fraction of the shortening and slowly loses accuracy
elsewhere.

### Experiment 3: marginal token analysis (your idea: the model judges its own words)

*The idea.* For every word of the thinking, remove only that word and measure how much the
model's certainty in the right answer drops. Words that do not matter get removed first.

*What happened.* Round 1: the model's answers shrank **2.6x more** than with the generic
filler detector for the same amount of trimming (12.1% vs 4.6% shorter), and it stays within
the limit with 4 answers per question (5.3% shorter, -2.0 pts). So the model learns its own
deletions better. But round 2 broke it (-8.8 pts, answers even longer): too much telegraphic
thinking makes it hesitate, especially on code. It costs about 85 minutes of GPU to score
every word once.

*Follow-up.* Applying the same word scoring to the easy questions only, with math kept whole,
did not shorten anything (round 1 level: 2.6% longer, -1.5 pts) or broke the model (round 2
level: 7.2% longer, -5.6 pts). So experiment 3's savings came from compacting the math, which
is also what costs math accuracy. **Removing whole useless paragraphs works; removing
scattered words makes text the model cannot learn to write.**

## What we learned

1. **Where the waste is depends on the task.** On easy and language questions most thinking
   is waste; on math every step carries the answer. A single model can learn both habits if
   the training data shows both.
2. **One big cut teaches laziness; the fix is not a better cut point but keeping math whole.**
   Every run that cut the math examples in one go (4 runs, across two sessions) lost 6-8
   points, with math -21 to -24.
3. **Measure with enough samples.** With 2 answers per question the average moves by about
   +/-4 points; two conclusions flipped once rechecked with 4 answers. The code loss of the
   winner looked like -8 with 4 answers and is -2.2 with 8.
4. **Delete paragraphs, not words.** Word-level trimming (experiments 2 and 3) gives
   telegraphic text; past one light round the model hesitates and writes more, not less.
5. **Code is the open problem.** We never train on code, and every method loses 5-10 points
   there even when the average holds.

## Suggested next steps

- Run the winner on the full evaluation (2,759 questions x 8 answers) before trusting it.
- Add code examples (kept whole, like math) to the training mix to protect MBPP+.
- Keep experiment 3's model-based scoring, but at the paragraph level: score each paragraph
  by the certainty lost without it, instead of each word.

## Honest caveats

- 240 questions, 2-4 answers each: a development signal, not the leaderboard score.
- The 4- and 8-answer rechecks share their first answers with the earlier tests (same seeds).
- Training used 450 practice questions from 3 sources (GSM8K, ARC-Easy, CommonsenseQA).


================================================================================================================
PART E. IDEA.md (verbatim, earlier write-up)
================================================================================================================

# IDEA: stop thinking once the answer is settled

## Motivation

Qwen3-0.6B often keeps thinking after it already knows its answer. If we can find, inside the
model's own reasoning, the point from which its answer no longer changes, everything after
that point is waste. Training the model on its own traces cut at that point should teach it to
stop there, without the blunt length penalties that also cut useful reasoning.

## Method: settle-point pruning + self-distillation

1. **Sample.** Generate the model's own replies to training questions (GSM8K, ARC-Easy,
   CommonsenseQA), with the evaluation's sampling settings.
2. **Probe.** Cut a reply's thinking after k tokens, close it with `</think>` and
   `Final answer:`, and read the model's probability of the correct answer by teacher
   forcing. Doing this every 16 tokens gives a curve p(correct | first k thinking tokens).
   All the cuts of one reply are packed into a single forward pass: each cut's closing tokens
   attend only to their own prefix, at the positions they would have there.
3. **Prune.** For each correct reply, split the thinking into paragraphs. Cut after the
   paragraph from which p(correct) stays above 0.9. Before that point, try deleting
   paragraphs that did not raise p(correct), keeping a deletion only if the whole edited trace
   still reaches 0.9. Let the model answer naturally from the edited thinking twice and keep
   the trace only if both answers are graded correct. Per question, keep the shortest accepted
   trace; questions that never settle keep their shortest correct original reply, so the model
   is not taught to always stop early.
4. **Self-distil.** LoRA fine-tune (rank 16, 2 epochs) on those traces, loss on the completion
   only.

The shortening is learned by the model; no prompt or decoding change is used at evaluation.

## Measurements

All on one free Colab T4. The full evaluation (2,759 questions x 8 samples) does not fit,
so both models are scored on the same subset: the first 40 questions of every benchmark in
`benchmarks.py`'s fixed shuffle, 2 samples each, with the evaluation's prompts, sampling and
token caps (`subset_questions.py`). Treat the numbers as a development signal.

**Where the thinking goes (probe, 100 validation questions x 4 samples).** Among correct
rollouts, 94% settle (p(correct) reaches 0.9 and stays) and 32% of their thinking comes
after that point. A quarter of all rollouts hit the 4,096-token cap, 86% of them on
Skywork math, which the model answers correctly only 4% of the time.

**Round 1.** 450 training questions (GSM8K, ARC-Easy, CommonsenseQA; 150 each, 2 samples,
2,048-token cap) gave 311 pruned traces, 75% shorter than the originals (578 -> 145 tokens).
LoRA SFT on all of them:

| | tokens saved | accuracy change |
|---|---|---|
| BFCL (3 subsets) | +20% to +34% | -11.3 to +1.3 pp |
| IFEval | +17% | -1.3 pp |
| MBPP+ | +61% | -5.0 pp |
| SVAMP | +43% | -15.0 pp |
| **average (geometric mean of tokens)** | **+39.1% [29.1, 50.2]** | **-6.25 pp [-10.7, -1.5]** |

The shortening transfers to every benchmark, including code and function calling that were
never trained on, but accuracy drops. Reading the SVAMP failures shows why: the model
learned to **underthink**. It now often closes its thinking right after restating the
question and writes the arithmetic in the answer line ("Final answer: 52 + 38 = 90"), which
the grader rejects, or skips the step that mattered. 244 of the 311 traces were
multiple-choice questions the model knows almost at once, so the data taught "stop
immediately".

**Round 2** (same rollouts): keep one paragraph after the settle point (`--margin 1`),
require 4 correct natural answers instead of 2 (`--check-samples 4`), and cap training at
70 traces per dataset (`--max-per-source 70`).

Rerun on an L4 in bf16, with its own base run on the same subset:

| | tokens saved | accuracy change |
|---|---|---|
| BFCL (3 subsets) | +32% to +39% | -10.0 to -3.8 pp |
| IFEval | +40% | +3.8 pp |
| MBPP+ | +86% | -6.3 pp |
| SVAMP | +49% | -23.8 pp |
| **average** | **+59.6% [51.2, 67.7]** | **-8.0 pp [-12.2, -4.0]** |

More shortening, no accuracy recovery: SVAMP gets worse. Imitating pruned traces teaches
"short" faster than it teaches "correct". The TokenProbe paper reports the same thing (SFT on
compressed traces degenerates), and both it and ShorterBetter train with RL from the base
model, where a wrong answer always loses reward. That is the next stage.

**Round 3: RL from the base model** (`rl_train.py`). Per question, 8 sampled replies.
Reply-level reward from ShorterBetter: correct replies are rewarded for being close to the
group's shortest correct length L* (the group mean when none is correct), wrong ones never
beat a correct one. Token-level pressure from TokenProbe: each reply's log-probs are z-scored,
the best 10-token windows are "core", and in replies over a budget (core length x a scale
annealed from 3 to 1.5) the other tokens lose their KL anchor to the base model and are pushed
down. A variant marks redundant tokens with our own probe instead (everything after p(correct)
settles).

RL loop 1 (12 steps x 32 questions x 8 samples from the base model, GSM8K/ARC/CSQA, KL
variant, alpha 0.5, lr 2e-5, LoRA r16, ~1h on an L4):

| | tokens saved | accuracy change |
|---|---|---|
| BFCL (3 subsets) | +4% to +12% | -5.0 to +8.8 pp |
| IFEval | +16% | -1.3 pp |
| MBPP+ | -18% (longer) | -3.8 pp |
| SVAMP | +13% | -1.3 pp |
| **average** | **+5.7% [-4.0, 14.9]** | **-1.2 pp [-4.8, 2.7]** |

Accuracy is preserved (within noise), unlike both SFT rounds, but 12 steps barely move the
length: the published runs use hundreds of steps.

ROUND3

## What did not work / limits

LIMITS

PART E. HANDOFF_MVT_FADING.md (verbatim, earlier write-up)

# Handoff: test "stop when thinking stops paying" + "fading worked examples"

A second Claude session runs this track while the first one keeps working on RL, steering and
AdaptThink on its own Colab runtime. The two must not share a runtime or a branch.

## The challenge

BottleCapAI ThinkingCap Test: make Qwen3-0.6B use fewer tokens (thinking included) without
losing accuracy. The hard constraint is **accuracy change >= -3 points** against the raw model.
The model must load in plain Transformers and vLLM with no custom code, so a LoRA or changed
weights are fine and a custom decoding loop is not.

## Where things are

- Repo: `github.com/arsenelemoigne/Test`. Base branch: `ccr-f7e14615-o4qu8r`. Code lives in `thinkingcap/`.
- **Create your own branch** from it (for example `mvt-fading`) and push only there. In your
  copy of `thinking_probe_colab.ipynb`, change `B=` / the wget URLs to your branch.
- Read these first:
  - `EXPERIMENT_LOG.md`: every run, its hyperparameters and its numbers.
  - `IDEA.md`: the method and why the SFT rounds failed.
- Benchmark harness: the challenge's `evaluate.py` and `compare.py`, run from the ThinkingCap-Test
  clone the notebook sets up.
- Evaluation subset: `subset_questions.py`, the first 40 questions per benchmark (240 in total),
  `--samples 2`.
- **Run the raw-model evaluation again on your own runtime** before comparing. Don't reuse
  numbers from another machine.

## Tools that already exist

| File | What it does |
|---|---|
| `probe_curve.py` | Samples replies. Every 16 thinking tokens it closes the thinking with `</think> Final answer:` and reads p(correct answer) by teacher forcing, all cuts packed into one forward pass. This gives the gain curve p(correct) vs tokens spent, per reply. Outputs: `rollouts.jsonl`, `probes.jsonl`. |
| `prune_traces.py` | Cuts each correct trace at the **settle point** (p >= 0.9 and stays), plus `--margin` paragraphs. Deletes flat paragraphs. Keeps a cut only if `--check-samples` natural answers from the cut trace are correct. Writes `sft.jsonl`. |
| `sft.py` | LoRA r16 SFT on completions (fp32 LoRA weights, `--max-per-source`). |
| `subset_questions.py`, `patch_evaluate_t4.py` | Evaluation subset; vLLM attention backend patch (TRITON_ATTN on a T4). |

## What already failed (read before designing)

SFT on cut traces made the model **underthink**:
- Round 1: +39% fewer tokens, -6.3 pts.
- Round 2: +60% fewer tokens, -8.0 pts; SVAMP -24 pts.

The model learned "stop right after restating the question". Most of the cut traces came from
easy multiple-choice questions it knew at once. Any SFT variant has to beat that failure, so
report SVAMP separately.

## Experiment 1: Marginal Value Theorem cut (Charnov 1976)

Treat thinking as foraging for certainty. g(t) is the probe curve p(correct | first t thinking
tokens). The MVT says to leave the patch when the marginal gain equals the average rate,
including a travel cost c (the answer tokens that follow anyway):

    stop at the first t where  g'(t) <= g(t) / (t + c)    (smooth g' over a few cuts)

This differs from the existing settle rule, which uses a fixed 0.9 threshold. Steps:

1. Implement it as `prune_traces.py --rule mvt --travel C`, alongside the settle rule.
2. Report how far the MVT cut point is from the settle point (median tokens, and the share of
   traces cut earlier vs later).
3. SFT on the cut traces, then evaluate. Keep the natural-answer check and the per-source cap.
4. Sweep c: a larger travel cost means staying longer.

## Experiment 2: fading worked examples (Renkl; Stepwise Internalization, Deng et al. 2024)

Remove reasoning gradually instead of one brutal cut, so the model absorbs the skipped steps:

1. Round k: from each correct trace, delete about 10% more of the thinking: the paragraphs with
   the smallest probe gain first (or the earliest ones, as a variant).
2. Keep a trace only if natural answers from the shortened trace are still correct.
3. SFT round k **starting from round k-1's adapter**, for about 5 rounds.
4. Evaluate after each round and stop at the last round within -3 pts.

This is the natural fix for the SFT failure above. MVT can set the final target length.

## Practical notes

- Colab Pro, L4, `DTYPE=bfloat16`.
- Editing a cell that is queued doesn't change what runs; interrupt and rerun.
- Each `!` line of a running cell stops with one click on its run button.
- Log every run (hyperparameters, data counts, per-benchmark tokens saved and accuracy change)
  in a new section of `EXPERIMENT_LOG.md` **on your branch**.
- Keep explanations to the user simple.

TASK: run the MVT (Marginal Value Theorem) cut on every data type, on data matched to the evaluation,
and report the gain per data type. You start with no context: everything you need is below.

=== 1. THE CHALLENGE ===
BottleCapAI ThinkingCap Test (github.com/BottleCapAI/ThinkingCap-Test): make Qwen3-0.6B write fewer
tokens (thinking included) while keeping accuracy. Our own limit: accuracy change >= -3 points vs the
raw model. Rules (README): start from Qwen3-0.6B; LoRA or changed weights are fine, but the model must
load in plain Transformers and vLLM (no custom code); do NOT train on the evaluation benchmarks; keep
the evaluation settings unchanged (prompt, sampling, TOKEN CAP: 4,096 tokens, 8,192 for MBPP+ code).
Evaluation: 4 benchmarks, each counts 25% of the score: SVAMP (math, 1,000 q), MBPP+ (code, 378 q),
BFCL (tool calls, 840 q in 3 parts: simple_python 400, multiple 200, irrelevance 240), IFEval
(instructions, 541 q). Full run = 2,759 questions x 8 samples. Accuracy change = mean over the 4
benchmarks; tokens saved = 1 - geometric mean of per-benchmark token ratios. Answers cut off by the
token cap count as wrong.

=== 2. WHERE THINGS ARE ===
Repo: github.com/arsenelemoigne/Test, folder thinkingcap/.
YOUR branch: mvt-pertype (already created). Push ONLY there. Never push to mvt-fading (another Claude
session works there) or to ccr-f7e14615-o4qu8r (a third session doing RL).
Read first, in this order:
  - thinkingcap/REPORT_MVT_FADING.md            plain-language summary of night 1
  - thinkingcap/EXPERIMENT_LOG.md               every run; read "Track 2" and "Track 2b" (the per-type grid)
  - thinkingcap/track.py                        the stage runner; read grid_data, grid, grid_evaluate, gridtable, combine
Key code: prune_traces.py (MVT rule: --rule mvt --travel C --unit paragraph|tokens32), probe_curve.py
(sampling, caps 4,096 and 8,192 for code), prepare_types.py (the data), typed.py (graders = the
evaluation's own), check_vllm.py (batched natural-answer check), sft.py (LoRA r16), merge.py, typed.py.

=== 3. WHAT MVT IS (short) ===
For each correct reply of the model, we measure after every paragraph (or every 32-token batch) how
sure the model is of its own correct answer (teacher forcing). The MVT says: stop thinking at the
first point where no later part of the thinking pays better per token than the average so far,
g(t)/(t + c). c ("travel cost") is patience: c=1024 stays longer, c=256 stops sooner. The cut trace is
kept only if the model, answering naturally from it twice, is still correct. Then LoRA SFT on the cut
traces, and evaluation.

=== 4. WHAT WE ALREADY KNOW (from mvt-fading, old data) ===
Per type, trained alone, 4 samples on the 240-question test sample (tokens saved / accuracy):
  tools   MVT paragraphs c=1024: +42% / -0.7   (free)        tokens32: +4% / -1.6
  code    MVT paragraphs c=1024: +58% / -3.4 (MBPP+ -13.7)   tokens32: +24% / -1.9 (MBPP+ -5.6)
  math    MVT paragraphs: +50% / -11.5 (SVAMP -16)  -> cutting math breaks it
  text    MVT paragraphs: +13% / -1.5                       tokens32: +5% / +0.6
  Training on one kind's own whole replies ("whole") already costs 2-3.5 points: compare each MVT
  run to the "whole" run of the same type, not only to the raw model.
  Combining every type's best cut in one training makes the cuts ADD UP (-6.2): see "Combined models".
  Best model so far: "light mix" (multiple-choice + tool MVT cuts, math whole x2): +48.6% / -2.3.
Old data was mismatched: GSM8K much harder than SVAMP, Hermes tools unlike BFCL, a 2,048 cap.

=== 5. WHAT IS NEW: THE MATCHED DATA (TRACK_SET=matched) ===
  math   gsm8k_all  GSM8K train+test, filtered to questions with a correct reply <= 700 tokens (SVAMP-like)
  code   mbpp_train MBPP tasks minus every MBPP+ task
  tool   toolace    ToolACE: BFCL's function format and call syntax, BFCL's mix (48% one function,
                    24% several, 28% no function fits); names with spaces become identifiers
  text   ifeval_like argilla/ifeval-like-data (filtered), graded by IFEval's own verifiers
  choice arc_easy + commonsense_qa (not in the evaluation; their cuts spill over usefully)
300 questions per source, 2 replies each, caps 4,096 and 8,192 for code. Do NOT use ASDiv, MAWPS,
MultiArith or Orca-Math: SVAMP was built from ASDiv/MAWPS (rule 2). The other session builds the same
questions (same seed) with 8 replies each for "shortest of 8"; keep --seed 0 so both match.

=== 6. HOW TO RUN ===
COLAB LIMIT: the account allows only 2 GPU sessions at once and both may already be in use (the
mvt-fading full evaluation and the RL session's thinking_probe_colab). Ask the user which one may be
stopped, or to use another Google account. Never terminate a session without the user's yes.
Open https://colab.research.google.com/github/arsenelemoigne/Test/blob/mvt-pertype/thinkingcap/mvt_pertype_colab.ipynb
(L4 is preset), Runtime -> Run all. It sets TRACK_BRANCH=mvt-pertype and TRACK_SET=matched, installs,
then the last cell works through thinkingcap/plan_mvt.txt from YOUR branch, rereading it after every
stage: push a line to queue more, no restart needed. Stages skip finished work.
plan_mvt.txt already holds: base, recheck (4-sample base), grid_data, then for every type
(tool, text, choice, code, math): whole, MVT c=1024 and c=256, by paragraphs and by 32-token batches,
then gridtable. About 25 runs x 15-20 min. Reorder or trim it if the budget is short.
Compute: an L4 burns ~3.1 Colab units per hour; check the balance under Runtime -> View resources.
Colab wipes the runtime disk when it disconnects (idle or when the tab is closed): log every result
in EXPERIMENT_LOG.md on your branch as soon as it appears. Reading results: the cell output can stop
streaming after a reconnect; then open /content/track_log.txt from the Files panel (refresh the panel
first) and read its text.
First real use of the ToolACE parser on the GPU: check the grid_data output ("toolace simple/multiple/
irrelevance: N usable") and fix prepare_types.py on your branch if it fails.

=== 7. WHAT TO REPORT ===
For every type: tokens saved and accuracy change on its OWN benchmark and on the other three
(spill-over), vs the raw model AND vs that type's "whole" run; which MVT setting (c, unit) is best
per type. `python track.py gridtable` prints the matrix. Then build one model from the per-type
winners with `track.py combine` (pool their sft.jsonl rows; remember cuts add up, start light), score
it with 4 samples, and log it. Explain results to the user simply (they want plain language, no
jargon). Add a "Track 3: MVT per type, matched data" section to EXPERIMENT_LOG.md on mvt-pertype.

=== 8. CONVENTIONS ===
Git commits end with: Co-Authored-By: Claude Opus 5.5 <noreply@anthropic.com>
Hard rule: accuracy within -3 points of the raw model on the same questions and samples.
Small test samples (40 q x 4) swing by about +/-4 points: say so when a difference is within that.

PART E. README.md (verbatim, earlier write-up)

# ThinkingCap Test: thinking probe and trace pruning

Tools for the [BottleCapAI ThinkingCap Test](https://github.com/BottleCapAI/ThinkingCap-Test):
make Qwen3-0.6B think shorter without losing accuracy.

| File | What it does |
|---|---|
| `probe_curve.py` | Samples answers, then reads p(correct answer) every few thinking tokens by forcing `</think>` and scoring each candidate answer. Shows how much thinking comes after the answer is already settled. |
| `prune_traces.py` | Turns correct rollouts into shorter ones: cuts after p(correct) settles, tries deleting steps that add nothing, and keeps a trace only if the model still answers it correctly on its own. Writes SFT targets. |
| `sft.py` | LoRA fine-tune on those targets; the adapter loads into the test's `evaluate.py --adapter`. |
| `thinking_probe_colab.ipynb` | Runs the above on a Colab GPU. |
| `tests/test_pipeline.py` | Unit tests plus an end-to-end test on a tiny toy model (CPU, under a minute). |
| `docs/thinking-reduction-research.md` | Literature survey and method proposal. |
| `docs/toy_curve.png` | The probe curve on the toy model. |

## Run

The scripts import the test repo's `utils/`, so copy them into a ThinkingCap-Test checkout:

```bash
git clone https://github.com/BottleCapAI/ThinkingCap-Test && cd ThinkingCap-Test
uv pip install -r requirements.txt
cp /path/to/thinkingcap/*.py . && cp /path/to/thinkingcap/tests/test_pipeline.py tests/
python utils/train/prepare_data.py
python probe_curve.py --out runs/probe                                  # add --dtype float16 on a T4
python prune_traces.py --rollouts runs/probe/rollouts.jsonl --out runs/prune
python sft.py --data runs/prune/sft.jsonl --out adapters/pruned-sft
python evaluate.py --out runs/pruned-sft --adapter adapters/pruned-sft
python compare.py runs/base runs/pruned-sft
```

Tests: `python -m pytest tests/test_pipeline.py -q` (needs torch, transformers, peft, matplotlib, pytest).

## Status

- Tested only on a tiny toy model: the code is checked, the method is not yet measured on Qwen3-0.6B.
- The vLLM sampling step in `probe_curve.py` has not been run yet (it needs a GPU).
- Not built yet: the "select shortest correct" mode, conformal calibration of the cut threshold,
  and the RL stage with a Lagrangian accuracy constraint.

# Extra stages for the overnight run: track.py reads this from the branch after every stage.

mix --mc runs/mvtf/fade-gain-marginal/r2/sft.jsonl --math runs/mvtf/prune-mvth1024-full-gsm8k/sft.jsonl --repeat gsm8k:2 --name marginal2-mathwhole-x2
recheck --samples 8 --adapter adapters/mvth1024-full-gsm8k-xgsm8k2

grid --type tool --method mvt --travel 1024 --unit tokens32

grid --type tool --method fade --rounds 3
grid --type text --method fade --rounds 3

# the final model: best method per kind (MC, code and tools cut by MVT, math whole x2)
rollouts
mvt --travel 1024 --full-sources gsm8k
combine --name final --inputs runs/mvtf/prune-mvth1024-full-gsm8k/sft.jsonl:arc_easy:commonsense_qa runs/grid/math-whole/sft.jsonl:gsm8k runs/grid/code-mvt1024-tokens32/sft.jsonl:mbpp_train runs/grid/tool-mvt1024/sft.jsonl:hermes_tools runs/grid/text-mvt1024/sft.jsonl:ifeval_like --repeat gsm8k:2 --max-per-source 150

stats --inputs mvtf/s4-base grid/probe/rollouts.jsonl mvtf/probe/rollouts.jsonl

merge --name full --inputs adapters/mvth1024-full-gsm8k-xgsm8k2:1 adapters/grid/tool-mvt1024:1 adapters/grid/code-mvt1024-tokens32:1 adapters/grid/math-fade/r2:1
# the full evaluation (2,759 questions x 8 samples) of the best candidate within -2.5 pts on the 4-sample subset

# shortest-of-8 self-distillation on data matched to the evaluation, checked on the real test sample

# "be brief" steering: directions A/B/C from the best-of-8 replies, small push tests, fresh held-out replies

# bigger per-kind direction reading (every reply, no generation)
steer_read

# steering push tests again: a check that compares the push's effect in both engines, stronger pushes

# (dropped: DPO on the same replies already works) bon --name r2 --sft-lr 1e-4 --sft-epochs 3 --min-saved 0.25 --per-source 300 --max-per-source 300

# Runtime 2 (second_colab.ipynb): calibrated cuts (confidence vs real outcome) + RL. Every block is saved to Drive
# and quickly scored on the choosing set (seed 20261023), so any point can be gone back to.
# Earlier lines of this queue (ca1, ca2, rep4, rep5) are done; their results are on Drive and in EXPERIMENT_LOG.md.
# 1. smoke tests: tiny sizes, no scoring, check that every step runs
rlcal --name rcs --start soup:ca1 --data-seed 31 --smoke
rlcal --name rcs4 --start models/rep4-blend50 --data-seed 32 --rl-only --smoke
# 2. on ca1 (clean, unseen +48.6% / -1.8): calibrated cuts at 1-80% of the thinking -> DPO 3 seeds -> RL 120 steps in blocks of 20
rlcal --name rc1 --start soup:ca1 --data-seed 31 --gen-questions 1000 --kod-questions 300 --floor -1.5
# smoke test of the RL-only path again, after the fix
rlcal --name rcs4 --start models/rep4-blend50 --data-seed 32 --rl-only --smoke --rl-block 1
# rc1 again (fixed RL question pool, 23 Oct): every finished part is skipped, so this only redoes what failed
rlcal --name rc1 --start soup:ca1 --data-seed 31 --gen-questions 1000 --kod-questions 300 --floor -1.5 --rl-block 20
# 3. (shared with runtime 1: whichever runtime gets here first runs it) RL only, on the round 4 50/50 blend (unseen +57.3% / -2.4, MBPP grey zone): the closest to >55% / -2
# rc1 with the stopping rule per type (23 Oct, 03:50): the calibration showed ca1's confidence is too poorly
# calibrated for math, code, tools and instructions, so those types are cut where the 20 re-answers are all right
rlcal --name rc1 --start soup:ca1 --data-seed 31 --gen-questions 1000 --kod-questions 300 --floor -1.5 --rl-block 20 --seeds 0 1 2
rlcal --name rc1 --start soup:ca1 --data-seed 31 --gen-questions 1000 --kod-questions 300 --floor -1.5 --rl-block 20 --seeds 0 1 2 --rl-alpha 0.15
# 23 Oct ~09:30, user's call: every rc1 version scored straight on the unseen set x8 (no choosing set): RL blocks first,
# then the DPO soups 10 and 20 (soup 30 already scored)
fresh --inputs models/rc1-rl-20 models/rc1-rl-40 models/rc1-rl-60 models/rc1-soup-10 models/rc1-soup-20
shared rlcal --name rr4 --start models/rep4-blend50 --data-seed 32 --gen-questions 1000 --kod-questions 300 --floor -1.5 --rl-only
# 23 Oct ~06:30: RL ran out of GPU memory on long replies (vocabulary-wide log-probs for every position): fixed by
# chunked, recomputed log-probs. rc1's RL resumes from its saved block 1 (step 20); then rr4 restarts cleanly.
rlcal --name rc1 --start soup:ca1 --data-seed 31 --gen-questions 1000 --kod-questions 300 --floor -1.5 --rl-block 20 --seeds 0 1 2 --rl-alpha 0.15 --rl-steps 120
# 23 Oct ~09:00: RL stopped after 3 blocks (saving 55 -> 54 -> 51 -> 48.5% on the choosing set, accuracy flat, thinking
# rising to 400-500 tokens on the repeated questions): choose among the DPO soup and blocks 1-3, report x8
rlcal --name rc1 --start soup:ca1 --data-seed 31 --gen-questions 1000 --kod-questions 300 --floor -1.5 --rl-block 20 --seeds 0 1 2 --rl-alpha 0.15 --rl-steps 60
# rr4 on fresh questions (each seen about once over 120 RL steps), 23 Oct ~08:15
# 23 Oct ~10:15, user's call: clean experiments on the rc1 line first, every result on the fresh set x8
# 1. calibration re-check: rc1 after the pairs step, then after RL 40, on the very same cuts as ca1's table
calcheck --name rc1s30 --calcheck-model models/rc1-soup-30 --calcheck-from rc1
calcheck --name rc1rl40 --calcheck-model models/rc1-rl-40 --calcheck-from rc1
# 2. RL done right on rc1 after the pairs step: fresh contamination-checked questions (each seen ~once), alpha 0.3,
#    every 20-step block scored on the fresh set x8
rlcal --name rr1 --start models/rc1-soup-30 --data-seed 44 --floor -1.5 --rl-only --rl-steps 120 --rl-fresh-seed 42 --rl-alpha 0.3 --judge fresh
# 3. second calibrated-cut round on rc1 RL 40 (pairs only, no RL); soups 10-90 scored on the fresh set x8
rlcal --name rc2 --start models/rc1-rl-40 --data-seed 43 --gen-questions 1000 --kod-questions 300 --floor -1.5 --rl-steps 0 --judge fresh --dpo-steps 10 20 30 60 90
# only if time is left: RL on the grey-zone round-4 blend
shared rlcal --name rr4 --start models/rep4-blend50 --data-seed 32 --gen-questions 1000 --kod-questions 300 --floor -1.5 --rl-only --rl-steps 120 --rl-fresh-seed 41 --rl-alpha 0.3
end

# rebuild after the runtime was recycled (2026-10-21 11:07); every result and adapter is saved to Google Drive
# best-of-8 data: a mixed half (750 of 1,500 questions) x 8, to save ~2 h and the gentle SFT start, no test (already scored: +4.4% / -2.8)
bon --per-source 300 --max-per-source 300 --skip-eval --gen-questions 750
# the winner again, pushed to ~130 steps to find where it breaks (was step-25 +47.6% / -2.6); quick 2-sample
# scores along the way, the best within the limit then with 4 samples
bon_dpo --dpo-epochs 4 --save-every 10 --eval-steps 10 20 30 50 70 100 130
# DPO straight from the raw model, no SFT step (same pairs, same settings)
bon_dpo --name bon-dpo-raw --start raw --dpo-epochs 2 --save-every 10 --eval-steps 10 20 30 40 60
# code fix: code gets only right-beats-wrong pairs, with a typical-length correct reply; intermediate scores
bon_dpo --name bon-dpo-code --no-shorten mbpp_train --dpo-epochs 2 --save-every 10 --eval-steps 10 20 30 40 60
# no code pairs at all (as Lightning McQwen3, who trained on no code): code changes only by spill-over
bon_dpo --name bon-dpo-nocode --drop-sources mbpp_train --dpo-epochs 2 --save-every 10 --eval-steps 10 20 30 40 60
# half data: one pass over the pairs is ~32 steps, so these runs take 2 passes
# every sweep: 2 samples per checkpoint, the best within the limit re-scored with 4
# push past 50%: round 2, the best checkpoint so far answers the 750 questions not used yet, 8 times each;
# new pairs, one pass of DPO from it (more passes undo the brevity)
bon_dpo --name bon-dpo-r2 --rollouts-from auto --fresh-questions --gen-questions 750 --dpo-epochs 1 --save-every 10 --eval-steps 10 20 30
# round 2 again on the same new answers, with the winning recipe (no code pairs): the running round 2 was
# queued before no-code won and keeps code pairs; the two compare code in vs out in round 2
bon_dpo --name bon-dpo-r2nc --rollouts-from adapters/bon-dpo-nocode/step-30 --probe-from bon-dpo-r2 --drop-sources mbpp_train --dpo-epochs 1 --save-every 10 --eval-steps 10 20 30
# round 2 is shorter (55-64%) but -4 points at 2 samples: check the two best with 4 samples
recheck --samples 4 --adapter adapters/bon-dpo-r2nc/step-10
recheck --samples 4 --adapter adapters/bon-dpo-r2/step-20
# a gentler round 2: more right-beats-wrong pairs (60%), checkpoints every 5 steps
bon_dpo --name bon-dpo-r2g --rollouts-from adapters/bon-dpo-nocode/step-30 --probe-from bon-dpo-r2 --drop-sources mbpp_train --mix 0.4,0.6,0 --dpo-epochs 1 --save-every 5 --eval-steps 5 10 15 20
# the gentle round 2 (step 10: +54.3% / -1.1 at 4 samples) confirmed with 8 answers per question
recheck --samples 8 --adapter adapters/bon-dpo-r2g/step-10
# round 3: a fresh draw of questions (seed 1, minus those used), answered 8 times by the gentle round-2 model;
# no code pairs, guard-heavy mix, checkpoints every 5 steps
bon_dpo --name bon-dpo-r3 --rollouts-from adapters/bon-dpo-r2g/step-10 --data-seed 1 --gen-questions 750 --drop-sources mbpp_train --mix 0.4,0.6,0 --dpo-epochs 1 --save-every 5 --eval-steps 5 10 15 20
# code is the bottleneck: how the best model's code answers fail (8 samples), then a round with code
# "stopped too early" pairs (the model's own correct code reply over its own early-cut wrong answer)
diag --name s8-bon-dpo-r2g-step-10 --dataset mbpp
code_round --name code-r3 --start adapters/bon-dpo-r2g/step-10 --dpo-epochs 1 --save-every 5 --eval-steps 5 10 15 20
# code round step 5: +53.2% / -0.1 at 4 samples; confirm with 8; then a bigger code dose (all ~600 code questions
# of both draws answered 4x by the start model, all their too-early pairs)
recheck --samples 8 --adapter adapters/code-r3/step-5
code_round --name code-r3b --start adapters/bon-dpo-r2g/step-10 --code-data data/bon.jsonl data/bon-seed1.jsonl --dpo-epochs 1 --save-every 5 --eval-steps 5 10 15 20
confirm --name code-r3b
# the frontier at 8 samples: r2g step 10 +54.2/-1.6, code-r3 step 5 +53.6/-2.9, code-r3b step 15 +60.9/-3.0.
# Round 3 without code pairs at 8 samples, then the midpoint of r2g and code-r3b (weight-space average of two
# checkpoints of one training line), at 8 samples
confirm --name bon-dpo-r3
merge --name soup-r2g-r3b --inputs adapters/bon-dpo-r2g/step-10:0.5 adapters/code-r3b/step-15:0.5 --samples 8
# round 4 from round 3 step 5 (+56.9 / -1.9 at 8): a third fresh draw (seed 2), no code pairs, gentle mix;
# its best checked with 8 samples
bon_dpo --name bon-dpo-r4 --rollouts-from adapters/bon-dpo-r3/step-5 --data-seed 2 --gen-questions 750 --drop-sources mbpp_train --mix 0.4,0.6,0 --dpo-epochs 1 --save-every 5 --eval-steps 5 10 15
confirm --name bon-dpo-r4
# (the read-only peek and the fresh-question test moved to the second runtime: plan_second.txt)
# round 5 from round 4's best (step 15), a fourth fresh draw (seed 3), same recipe, checked with 8 samples
bon_dpo --name bon-dpo-r5 --rollouts-from adapters/bon-dpo-r4/step-15 --data-seed 3 --gen-questions 750 --drop-sources mbpp_train --mix 0.4,0.6,0 --dpo-epochs 1 --save-every 5 --eval-steps 5 10 15
confirm --name bon-dpo-r5
# MBPP-free family B: as A plus clean code data, the MBPP-like slice of KodCode (right-beats-wrong pairs only)
clean --name clean-b1 --start raw --code-source kodcode --kod-questions 300 --mix 0.6,0.4,0 --dpo-epochs 1 --save-every 10 --eval-steps 10 20 30
confirm --name clean-b1
# as for A1: the later checkpoints of B1 scored with 4 samples before B2 picks its start (a missing one just fails)
recheck --samples 4 --adapter adapters/clean-b1/step-20
recheck --samples 4 --adapter adapters/clean-b1/step-30
clean --name clean-b2 --start auto:clean-b1 --code-source kodcode --kod-questions 300 --kod-exclude clean-b1 --gen-questions 750 --mix 0.4,0.6,0 --dpo-epochs 1 --save-every 5 --eval-steps 5 10 15
confirm --name clean-b2
fresh --inputs auto:clean-b1 auto:clean-b2
# (dropped by the user: the full-benchmark run comes only at the very end) full2 --inputs auto:clean-a2 auto:clean-b2 adapters/bon-dpo-r4/step-15 adapters/bon-dpo-r3/step-5
# round 5 on the fresh questions too (rounds 3 and 4 lost 1.5-2 points there)
fresh --inputs adapters/bon-dpo-r5/step-5
# last line: save everything to Drive, flush it, and stop (the notebook then releases the runtime)
end

# SECOND runtime (second_colab.ipynb, TRACK_SESSION=second). It only ADDS new names (adapters *-s1, runs f8-*

bon_dpo --name bon-dpo-r2g-s1 --rollouts-from adapters/bon-dpo-nocode/step-30 --probe-from bon-dpo-r2 --drop-sources mbpp_train --mix 0.4,0.6,0 --dpo-epochs 1 --save-every 5 --eval-steps 10 --dpo-seed 1
bon_dpo --name bon-dpo-r4-s1 --rollouts-from adapters/bon-dpo-r3/step-5 --probe-from bon-dpo-r4 --drop-sources mbpp_train --mix 0.4,0.6,0 --dpo-epochs 1 --save-every 5 --eval-steps 15 --dpo-seed 1

================================================================================================================
PART G. every script: docstring + command-line options with defaults (exact hyperparameters)
================================================================================================================

----- audit_contamination.py -----
Audit: every training question ever prepared, against every evaluation question (rule 2).

For each training question (the last user message of each row of the given data files), the share of
its word 8-grams found in one single evaluation question, and whether any 13-gram matches exactly.
Writes a report: per file and source, how many questions reach 30% / 50% overlap or share a 13-gram,
and the closest pairs side by side, so a person can judge them.

    python audit_contamination.py --data data/*.jsonl --out runs/audit

Options:
  parser.add_argument("--data", nargs="+", required=True, help="training data files (prepare_types.py rows)")
  parser.add_argument("--questions", default="questions/questions.jsonl")
  parser.add_argument("--out", required=True)

----- bon_pairs.py -----
DPO pairs from the best-of-N rollouts already sampled: no new generation.

Per question (only those kept by select_shortest.py and the contamination check):
  shorten  shortest correct reply  beats  the longest correct reply (at least --ratio times longer)
  guard    shortest correct reply  beats  the shortest wrong reply (finished, not cut by the cap):
           brevity must not come from giving up
  --drop-sources sources get no pairs at all (their length changes only by spill-over)
  --no-shorten sources get guard pairs only, with their median-length correct reply as chosen

    python bon_pairs.py --rollouts runs/bon/probe/rollouts.jsonl --keep runs/bon/select-r1/sft.jsonl --out runs/bon/pairs
    python dpo.py --pairs runs/bon/pairs/pairs.jsonl --init-adapter adapters/bon-r1 --out adapters/bon-dpo --mix 0.6,0.4,0

Options:
  parser.add_argument("--rollouts", required=True)
  parser.add_argument("--keep", required=True, help="sft.jsonl whose question ids are allowed")
  parser.add_argument("--out", required=True)
  parser.add_argument("--ratio", type=float, default=1.5)
  parser.add_argument("--drop-sources", default="", help="sources left out entirely (e.g. mbpp_train)")
  parser.add_argument("--min-correct", type=int, default=0, help="shorten pairs only for questions answered correctly at least this often (no lucky shortcuts)")
  parser.add_argument("--guard-median", default="", help="sources whose guard pairs use a median-length correct reply")
  parser.add_argument("--shorten-cap", nargs="*", default=[], help="source=N: at most N shorten pairs for that source")
  parser.add_argument("--length-match", type=float, default=0, help="right-beats-wrong only, between a right and a wrong reply at most this ratio apart in " "length (e.g. 1.25): correctness without any length signal; no short-beats-long pairs")
  parser.add_argument("--no-shorten", default="", help="sources that get guard pairs only (e.g. mbpp_train)")

----- cal_cuts.py -----
Calibrated cuts: where can the model's own correct replies stop thinking and still be right, per data type?

Confidence vs outcome: at every cut we also read the model's own confidence, the probability it gives (per token,
geometric mean) to the answer it finally wrote, right after "</think>" is closed at the cut (teacher forcing; works
for every type, no gold string needed). The summary compares that confidence with the real outcome (the share of
the 4 re-answers that are right), bin by bin (calibration table, ECE), and picks, per source, the confidence
threshold c: each reply stops at its earliest cut where confidence >= c; the lowest c whose real accuracy stays
within the allowance is kept. Pairs are built at the confidence-chosen cut (the model's own signal), validated by
the real re-answers; the outcome-only rule (tau) is reported beside it for comparison.

For each correct, finished reply (the model's own best-of-8 rollouts), the thinking is cut at --fractions of its
tokens (snapped back to the last paragraph break), "</think>" is closed there, and the model answers --samples
times from the cut (check_vllm.py, the model that wrote the rollouts). Each cut's success rate (right / samples) is
its measured confidence.

Calibration, per source: for each threshold tau in --taus, every reply stops at its earliest cut whose success rate
is >= tau (or keeps its full thinking when none is). The expected accuracy of a model that learned to stop there is
the mean success rate at the chosen cuts; its loss is 1 minus that, in points. The lowest tau whose loss stays within
the source's allowance (--allow, points; math is strict) is kept: the most saving at the accuracy we ask for.

Pairs, for DPO (dpo.py kinds):
  shorten   chosen = thinking cut at the calibrated point + "</think>" + a right answer written from there
            rejected = the full reply it was cut from (right but longer); only when the cut saves >= --min-save
  hard      chosen = the same calibrated cut + right answer; rejected = an earlier cut of the same reply + its
            wrong answer: where stopping becomes too early (teaches the line from both sides)

    python cal_cuts.py --rollouts runs/bon/probe-x/rollouts.jsonl --keep runs/bon/select-x/sft.jsonl \
        --model models/ca1-soup-10 --out runs/bon/cal-x

Options:
  parser.add_argument("--model", default="Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B")
  parser.add_argument("--adapter", help="the model that wrote the rollouts, as a LoRA")
  parser.add_argument("--rollouts", nargs="+", required=True)
  parser.add_argument("--keep", nargs="+", required=True, help="sft.jsonl files whose ids passed the contamination check")
  parser.add_argument("--out", required=True)
  parser.add_argument("--fractions", default="0.01,0.05,0.1,0.2,0.35,0.5,0.65,0.8", help="cut points, as shares of the reply's thinking (0.01: almost no thinking)")
  parser.add_argument("--taus", default="1.0,0.75,0.5")
  parser.add_argument("--allow", default="gsm_plus=0.5,gsm_ic=0.5,kodcode=1,default=2", help="source=points of expected accuracy loss allowed by the calibration")
  parser.add_argument("--samples", type=int, default=4)
  parser.add_argument("--per-question", type=int, default=1, help="correct replies cut per question (longest first)")
  parser.add_argument("--min-save", type=float, default=0.25)
  parser.add_argument("--answer-tokens", type=int, default=2048)
  parser.add_argument("--limit", type=int, default=0, help="smoke test: at most this many replies")
  parser.add_argument("--dtype", default="bfloat16")

----- check_vllm.py -----
Natural-answer check for many shortened traces at once, with vLLM.

Each input row is a prompt and a shortened thinking block; the model (optionally with a LoRA)
answers --samples times from it with the evaluation's sampling settings. A trace passes only if
every answer ends inside its token cap and is graded correct (typed.is_correct). Output: one
row per input with the shortest passing answer's token ids, or null.

    python check_vllm.py --inputs check_inputs.jsonl --out check_results.jsonl --samples 2

prune_traces.py and fade_traces.py call it with --checker vllm, in a child process, so the GPU
memory vLLM takes is released when it exits.

Options:
  parser.add_argument("--model", default="Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B")
  parser.add_argument("--adapter")
  parser.add_argument("--inputs", required=True)
  parser.add_argument("--out", required=True)
  parser.add_argument("--samples", type=int, default=2)
  parser.add_argument("--dtype", default="bfloat16")

----- code_pairs.py -----
Code "stopped too early" pairs: the model's own correct code replies against its own early-cut failures.

For each correct, finished code reply of the current model (its own rollouts), the thinking is cut
at --fractions of its tokens and the model answers --samples times from each cut (check_vllm.py,
one batch, the model's adapter). Where a cut yields a wrong answer, the pair is

    hard   the full reply (right)   beats   that cut + its wrong answer

using the latest such cut: the most tempting place to stop. These pairs teach code, specifically,
not to stop thinking too early (as on-policy truncation oracles do; Lightning McQwen3, ThoughtFold).

    python code_pairs.py --rollouts runs/bon/probe-r3/rollouts.jsonl --keep runs/bon/select-r3/sft.jsonl \
        --adapter adapters/start --out runs/bon/code-r3

Options:
  parser.add_argument("--model", default="Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B")
  parser.add_argument("--adapter", help="the model that wrote the rollouts")
  parser.add_argument("--rollouts", required=True)
  parser.add_argument("--keep", required=True, help="sft.jsonl whose ids passed the contamination check")
  parser.add_argument("--out", required=True)
  parser.add_argument("--source", default="mbpp_train")
  parser.add_argument("--fractions", default="0.25,0.5,0.75")
  parser.add_argument("--samples", type=int, default=4)
  parser.add_argument("--per-question", type=int, default=2)
  parser.add_argument("--answer-tokens", type=int, default=2048)
  parser.add_argument("--dtype", default="bfloat16")

----- contamination.py -----
Check training questions against the evaluation's questions before training on them (rule 2).

A training question is flagged when at least --share of its word 8-grams appear in one single
evaluation question: a near copy, not just shared boilerplate (BFCL's system prompt and IFEval's
constraint wordings are shared by design, so only the last user message is compared). Flagged
rows are dropped from the SFT file and listed.

    python contamination.py --data runs/bon/select/sft.jsonl --rollouts runs/bon/probe/rollouts.jsonl

Options:
  parser.add_argument("--data", required=True, help="sft.jsonl to clean in place")
  parser.add_argument("--rollouts", required=True, help="rollouts.jsonl holding each id's question text")
  parser.add_argument("--questions", default="questions/questions.jsonl")
  parser.add_argument("--share", type=float, default=0.5)

----- dpo.py -----
DPO on (chosen, rejected) reply pairs, continuing a LoRA, with an NLL anchor and a collapse tripwire.

    python dpo.py --pairs runs/r1/pairs/pairs.jsonl --init-adapter adapters/start --out adapters/r1

loss = -log sigmoid(beta * [(pi_c - ref_c) - (pi_r - ref_r)]) + nll * (mean token NLL of chosen)

pi and ref are summed log-probabilities of the reply tokens (thinking and answer) under the
policy and under the starting adapter; the reference is scored once before training. The NLL
anchor on the chosen reply (as in RPO, Pang et al. 2024) stops DPO from widening the margin by
lowering both sides. Pair kinds are mixed to --mix (shorten, guard, hard).

Every --save-every optimizer steps a checkpoint is saved. The tripwire watches held-out chosen
replies: if the probability that the reply ends right after </think> rises above --tripwire, a
collapse known from on-policy truncation DPO, training stops and the last checkpoint is kept.

Options:
  parser.add_argument("--model", default="Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B")
  parser.add_argument("--pairs", required=True)
  parser.add_argument("--init-adapter", required=True, help="the starting LoRA; also the DPO reference")
  parser.add_argument("--out", required=True)
  parser.add_argument("--mix", default="0.4,0.4,0.2", help="shares of shorten, guard, hard pairs")
  parser.add_argument("--beta", type=float, default=0.1)
  parser.add_argument("--nll", type=float, default=1.0, help="weight of the NLL anchor on the chosen reply")
  parser.add_argument("--lr", type=float, default=2e-5)
  parser.add_argument("--batch", type=int, default=16, help="pairs per optimizer step")
  parser.add_argument("--epochs", type=int, default=1)
  parser.add_argument("--max-steps", type=int, default=0, help="stop early (0: the whole epoch)")
  parser.add_argument("--save-every", type=int, default=25)
  parser.add_argument("--log-every", type=int, default=5)
  parser.add_argument("--held-out", type=int, default=48, help="pairs kept aside for the tripwire")
  parser.add_argument("--tripwire", type=float, default=0.2)
  parser.add_argument("--max-len", type=int, default=9000)
  parser.add_argument("--dtype", default="bfloat16")
  parser.add_argument("--seed", type=int, default=0)

----- fade_traces.py -----
Fading worked examples: shorten the model's own correct traces a little more every round.

One brutal cut (prune_traces.py) taught the model to stop right after restating the question.
Here the reasoning is removed gradually, as in fading worked examples (Renkl) and Stepwise
Internalization (Deng et al. 2024), so each round the model only has to absorb a few skipped
steps:

  1. Per question, take its shortest correct rollout and split the thinking into steps
     (paragraphs). Score p(gold) after every step once, with the base model (cached).
  2. Round k deletes steps until about k x --step of the thinking tokens are gone: the steps
     with the smallest probe gain first (--order gain), or the earliest ones (--order early).
     With --floor-travel C it never goes below the Marginal Value Theorem cut for travel cost
     C (prune_traces.mvt_index): MVT sets the final target length.
  3. Inside the steps that remain, compact the words (TokenSkip, Xia et al. 2025): a
     token classifier trained to tell filler from content (LLMLingua-2) scores every word,
     and round k keeps the best 1 - k x --word-step of them. Words with digits or math
     symbols always stay. "Okay, so 3 bags of 12 apples, that means 3 times 12 is 36"
     becomes something like "3 bags 12 apples 3 times 12 is 36".
     --word-scorer marginal scores each word with our own model instead (marginal token
     analysis): delete that one word from the whole trace and measure how much log p(gold)
     drops. A word the answer does not need scores about 0 and goes first.
  4. The current model (--adapter, round k-1's LoRA) answers naturally from the shortened
     thinking --check-samples times. The trace is kept only if every answer is correct;
     otherwise the question keeps its round k-1 trace (--previous), so hard questions stay
     long instead of dropping out and leaving only the easy ones. Before falling back it
     tries once more with round k-1's word compaction.

    python fade_traces.py --rollouts runs/probe/rollouts.jsonl --out runs/fade/r1 --round 1
    python sft.py --data runs/fade/r1/sft.jsonl --out adapters/fade-r1 --epochs 1
    python fade_traces.py --rollouts runs/probe/rollouts.jsonl --out runs/fade/r2 --round 2 \
        --adapter adapters/fade-r1 --previous runs/fade/r1/sft.jsonl --cache runs/fade/r1/curves.jsonl
    python sft.py --data runs/fade/r2/sft.jsonl --out adapters/fade-r2 --epochs 1 --init-adapter adapters/fade-r1

Options:
  parser.add_argument("--model", default="Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B")
  parser.add_argument("--adapter", help="the current learner (round k-1's LoRA): it scores the natural-answer check")
  parser.add_argument("--rollouts", required=True, help="rollouts.jsonl from probe_curve.py")
  parser.add_argument("--out", required=True)
  parser.add_argument("--round", type=int, required=True, help="k: delete about k x --step of the thinking")
  parser.add_argument("--step", type=float, default=0.1, help="share of the thinking removed per round")
  parser.add_argument("--order", choices=("gain", "early"), default="gain", help="gain: smallest probe gain first; early: earliest steps first")
  parser.add_argument("--previous", help="round k-1's sft.jsonl: the fallback when a trace fails the check")
  parser.add_argument("--cache", help="an earlier round's curves.jsonl, to skip rescoring the base curves")
  parser.add_argument("--floor-travel", type=float, help="never shorten below the MVT cut for this travel cost")
  parser.add_argument("--word-step", type=float, default=0.1, help="share of the words compacted away per round inside kept steps; 0 turns it off")
  parser.add_argument("--min-keep", type=float, default=0.3, help="never keep fewer than this share of words")
  parser.add_argument("--word-scorer", choices=("lingua", "marginal"), default="lingua", help="lingua: LLMLingua-2's filler classifier; marginal: log p(gold) lost without the word")
  parser.add_argument("--word-model", default="microsoft/llmlingua-2-xlm-roberta-large-meetingbank")
  parser.add_argument("--min-steps", type=int, default=1)
  parser.add_argument("--sources", type=lambda text: set(text.split(",")), default=set(), help="only these datasets' questions (default: all)")
  parser.add_argument("--easy-tokens", type=int, default=0, help="keep only questions with a correct rollout at most this long (0: all)")
  parser.add_argument("--unit", default="paragraph", help="paragraph, sentence, or tokensN (e.g. tokens32)")
  parser.add_argument("--checker", choices=("hf", "vllm"), default="hf")
  parser.add_argument("--check-samples", type=int, default=2)
  parser.add_argument("--answer-tokens", type=int, default=512)
  parser.add_argument("--batch-tokens", type=int, default=32768)
  parser.add_argument("--dtype", default="bfloat16")

----- merge.py -----
Merge several LoRA adapters into the base weights, each at its own strength.

W = W0 + sum_i w_i * dW_i, where dW_i is adapter i's update. Each adapter was trained on one kind of
data (tool calls, code, math...), so its update stays its own and can be turned down if it spills
onto the other kinds, instead of pooling all the shortened traces into one training run, where
the cuts add up. The result is a plain model directory (no adapter), which Transformers and vLLM
load without custom code:

    python merge.py --out models/merged --adapters adapters/grid/tool-mvt1024:1.0 adapters/grid/math-fade/r2:0.5
    python evaluate.py --model models/merged --out runs/merged

Options:
  parser.add_argument("--model", default="Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B")
  parser.add_argument("--adapters", nargs="+", required=True, help="path:weight, e.g. adapters/x:0.5")
  parser.add_argument("--out", required=True)
  parser.add_argument("--dtype", default="bfloat16", help="dtype of the saved weights")

----- mvt_pairs.py -----
On-policy, verified MVT cuts turned into DPO pairs.

For each correct reply of the CURRENT model (its own rollouts, sampled fresh each round):

  1. Split the thinking into units (paragraphs, or 32-token batches for code) and score the
     probe curve: p(correct answer | first t thinking tokens), as prune_traces.py does.
  2. Candidate cuts: the Marginal Value Theorem cut, one earlier (half as much thinking kept)
     and one later (halfway to the end). One rule-chosen cut and two neighbours instead of
     trying every 10%.
  3. Verify every candidate: the model answers --check-samples times from the cut thinking
     (check_vllm.py, one batch). A cut is safe only if every answer is right.
  4. Pairs, as in on-policy truncation oracles (Lightning McQwen3, ThoughtFold):
       shorten  the shortest safe cut (+ its own answer)  beats  the full reply
       guard    the shortest safe cut                     beats  a shorter unsafe cut + its wrong answer
       hard     no safe cut: the full reply               beats  the earliest cut + its wrong answer
     Maths (--strict-sources) only accepts cuts keeping at least --strict-keep of the thinking.

    python mvt_pairs.py --rollouts runs/r1/probe/rollouts.jsonl --adapter adapters/start --out runs/r1/pairs
    python dpo.py --pairs runs/r1/pairs/pairs.jsonl --init-adapter adapters/start --out adapters/r1

Options:
  parser.add_argument("--model", default="Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B")
  parser.add_argument("--adapter", help="the current model's LoRA: it scores, re-answers and wrote the rollouts")
  parser.add_argument("--rollouts", required=True, help="the current model's own rollouts (probe_curve.py)")
  parser.add_argument("--out", required=True)
  parser.add_argument("--travel", type=float, default=1024)
  parser.add_argument("--units", nargs="*", default=["mbpp_train=tokens32"], help="source=unit; paragraph for the rest")
  parser.add_argument("--check-samples", type=int, default=4, help="re-answers per cut; all must be right")
  parser.add_argument("--per-question", type=int, default=2, help="correct rollouts used per question")
  parser.add_argument("--strict-sources", default="gsm8k_all", help="sources whose cuts keep --strict-keep")
  parser.add_argument("--strict-keep", type=float, default=0.5, help="share of the thinking a strict cut keeps")
  parser.add_argument("--batch-tokens", type=int, default=32768)
  parser.add_argument("--dtype", default="bfloat16")

----- mvt_report.py -----
Where the Marginal Value Theorem cut falls against the settle cut, for several travel costs.

the thinking tokens after each step), so the sweep needs no GPU:

    python mvt_report.py runs/prune-mvt256/log.jsonl --travel 32,128,256,512,1024,2048

----- patch_evaluate_t4.py -----
Let evaluate.py run on a T4: choose vLLM's attention backend with an environment variable.

vLLM picks FlashInfer on a T4 and its prefill kernel fails there ("BatchPrefillWithPagedKVCache
failed with error invalid argument"). This adds one optional argument to the engine in
evaluate.py, read from EVAL_ATTENTION_BACKEND; prompts, sampling and token caps are untouched,
so scores stay comparable as long as the baseline is run the same way.

    python patch_evaluate_t4.py
    EVAL_ATTENTION_BACKEND=TRITON_ATTN python evaluate.py --out runs/base

Options:
  (none)
